
# BATCH 21: LED Patterns 2 (Projects 0201-0210)

## 1. Project 0201: Introduction to LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore modular code organization using functions. Learn how to wrap a common action (toggling an LED) into a reusable block, making the main execution loop cleaner and easier to read.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Functions/Subroutines**: Creating a named block of code that can be called multiple times.
*   **Modular Programming**: Organizing logic into manageable pieces.
*   **Code Reusability**: Reducing repetition by using function definitions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 LED + resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Blink LED** | GP15 | Controlled via toggle function |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Functions, drag `to [function_name]`** (define function)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)
*   **from Functions, drag `[function_name]`** (call function)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: This project focuses on functional structure.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Function Definition**
1.  **Create the Block**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag the `to [do something]` block.
    *   Change the name `do something` to `toggle_led`.
2.  **Define Behavior**:
    *   Inside the function block:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **1** second.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **1** second.

**B. Execution Loop**
3.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
4.  **Call Function**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag the `toggle_led` block (created automatically).
    *   Snap it inside the `pico_forever` loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Structure**: The Pico prepares the definition of "how to toggle" but doesn't run it yet.
2.  **Entry**: The code enters the forever loop.
3.  **Request**: The loop "calls" the `toggle_led` function.
4.  **Action**: The Pico jumps to the function code, blinks the LED for 2 seconds, then jumps back to the loop.
5.  **Repeat**: The loop immediately calls the function again.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

# Define Function
def toggle_led():
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(1)
    led.value(0)
    time.sleep(1)

# Main Loop
while True:
    toggle_led()
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Recursive Calling**: calling `toggle_led` inside the `toggle_led` definition will cause the Pico to run out of memory.
*   **Infinite Loop inside Function**: if you put a `forever` loop inside the function, the main loop will never get a chance to run again.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Parameters**: modify the function to accept a "speed" input so you can blink at different rates.
*   **Multi-LED**: Create a `toggle_red()` and `toggle_green()` and call them both in the main loop.

---

## 1. Project 0202: Blinking LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore rhythmic timing sequences (The Double Strobe). Learn how to create complex visual "signatures" by combining very fast pulses with long pauses, simulating specialized industrial or nautical signals.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Strobe Signatures**: Creating a unique blink pattern (Pulse-Pulse-Wait).
*   **Timing Precision**: Using 0.1s (100ms) delays for rapid transitions.
*   **Asymmetric Duty Cycle**: Having very long "Off" times compared to "On" times.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 LED + resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Strobe LED** | GP15 | Dual-pulse output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: All timing is fixed in the logic blocks.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Sequence Logic**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Pulse 1**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **0.1** seconds.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **0.1** seconds.
3.  **Pulse 2**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **0.1** seconds.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **LOW**.
4.  **Reset Delay**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Set to **2.0** seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Flash**: The Pico performs two extremely fast blinks (100ms each).
2.  **Pause**: The LED goes dark for 2 full seconds.
3.  **Cycle**: The pattern repeats, creating a professional-looking "Nautical Beacon" or "Emergency Strobe" effect.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # First Pulse
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.1)
    led.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.1)
    
    # Second Pulse
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.1)
    led.value(0)
    
    # Standard 2s Wait
    time.sleep(2.0)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Intermediate Wait**: If you don't wait between the first LOW and second HIGH, the LED will just look like it stayed ON for 200ms instead of blinking twice.
*   **Units**: Mixing up seconds (0.1) and milliseconds (100).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Triple Strobe**: Add a third pulse to the sequence.
*   **Sync Blinking**: Add a second LED that blinks in the gap when the first LED is waiting for 2 seconds.

## 1. Project 0203: Manual LED Patterns Control

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement gated execution (Hold-to-Blink). Learn how to link a sensor input (Button) as a prerequisite for a repeating logic block, ensuring that an action only occurs while a condition remains active.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Gated Logic**: Requiring a physical signal to "unlock" an automatic process.
*   **Nested Loops**: placing a repeating blink sequence inside an IF statement.
*   **Input Poling**: continuously checking a pin status to determine current behavior.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Button
*   LED + Resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Input Button** | GP14 | Hold to enable blinking |
| **Indicator LED** | GP15 | Blinks only when button is down |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (if/then/else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin control)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: The code reads the pin state directly.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Safety Output**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **LOW**.

**B. Gated Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Input Check**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag a `controls_if` block with **Else**.
    *   **Condition**: From **Smart IO**, check if `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.
4.  **Active State (Held)**:
    *   Inside the **If** block:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.2** seconds.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.2** seconds.
5.  **Idle State (Released)**:
    *   Inside the **Else** block:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: The Pico checks the button. Since it is released, the "Else" block ensures the LED is off.
2.  **Activation**: The user presses and holds the button.
3.  **Process**: The "If" condition becomes true. The Pico executes one blink cycle.
4.  **Continuity**: As long as the button stays down, the loop immediately goes back into the "If" block.
5.  **Termination**: The user lets go. The Pico switches to the "Else" branch and the LED stays dark.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        # Action only while held
        led.value(1)
        time.sleep(0.2)
        led.value(0)
        time.sleep(0.2)
    else:
        # Default safety state
        led.value(0)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wait in Else**: putting a wait in the Else block will make the button feel "unresponsive" when you first press it. Keep the idle branch fast.
*   **Inverted Logic**: if using a pull-up resistor, the logic will be backwards (LED blinks when NOT pressed).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Pulse Speed**: Change the wait times to 0.05s for a very fast buzzing light effect.
*   **Secondary Signal**: Add a second LED that is ON when the button is NOT pressed, and turns OFF when the blinking starts.

## 1. Project 0204: LED Patterns Sequences

### 2. Learning Objective
Learn about linear animations (The Chaser). Understand how to time multiple outputs to create the illusion of physical motion across a series of fixed light sources.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Shift Sequencing**: Turning one output off exactly when the next turns on.
*   **Perceptual Motion**: the visual effect of a "moving" dot created by sequential blinking.
*   **Array Control**: logically ordering GP pins to match their physical position.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   3 LEDs + Resistors
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED 1 (Left)** | GP15 | First in line |
| **LED 2 (Center)** | GP14 | Second in line |
| **LED 3 (Right)** | GP13 | Third in line |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Sequential logic.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Animation Loop**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Step 1 (Left)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP14** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP13** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.3** s.
3.  **Step 2 (Center)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP14** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.3** s.
4.  **Step 3 (Right)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP14** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** to **HIGH**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.3** s.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Step 1**: Only the first LED is on.
2.  **Transition**: The first LED turns off at the exact moment the second LED turns on.
3.  **Flow**: This continues to the third LED.
4.  **Loop**: The sequence jumps back to the first LED.
5.  **Result**: A "running light" effect that travels down the breadboard.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Hardware setup
l1 = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
l2 = Pin(14, Pin.OUT)
l3 = Pin(13, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # LED 1
    l1.value(1); l2.value(0); l3.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.3)
    
    # LED 2
    l1.value(0); l2.value(1); l3.value(0)
    time.sleep(0.3)
    
    # LED 3
    l1.value(0); l2.value(0); l3.value(1)
    time.sleep(0.3)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wiring Order**: If Pin 15 is connected to the right LED instead of the left, the light will move backwards.
*   **All-On**: Forgetting to set previous LEDs to LOW will result in all LEDs turning on and staying on.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Back and Forth**: Modify the sequence to go 1-2-3-2-1 (Knight Rider mode).
*   **Speed Dial**: add a potentiometer to change the 0.3s delay.

---

## 1. Project 0205: Interactive LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore frequency doubling through interaction. Learn how to use a variable-delay system where a button press "cuts the interval in half," creating a tension-building effect where the blinking speeds up progressively.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic Intervals**: using a variable to control `wait` time instead of a number.
*   **Division Logic**: Reducing a value by 50% with each event.
*   **Reset Thresholds**: Returning a variable to its default state after it exceeds a certain limit.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Button
*   LED + Resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Speed Button** | GP14 | Press to double speed |
| **Blink LED** | GP15 | Tempo indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (set variable)
*   **from Math, drag `math_arithmetic`** (division)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (checking speed limits)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **delay_time** (Number): Wait duration in seconds (Init: 1.0).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Set Startup Speed**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag `variables_set`. Name it `delay_time`.
    *   Set value to **1.0**.

**B. Main Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Speed Input**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. Condition: **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.
    *   Inside If: From **Variables**, set `delay_time` to **Math** (`delay_time` / 2).
    *   Inside If: from **Smart IO**, wait **0.3** s (Debounce).
4.  **Safety Reset**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. Condition: `delay_time` < 0.05.
    *   Inside If: From **Variables**, set `delay_time` to **1.0**.

**C. Dynamic Blink**
5.  **Execute Pattern**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Snap `delay_time` block into it.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_wait`. Snap `delay_time` block into it.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The LED blinks slowly (once per 2 seconds).
2.  **Tap**: The user clicks the button. `delay_time` becomes 0.5.
3.  **Reaction**: The LED immediately starts blinking twice as fast.
4.  **Escalation**: Another tap makes it 0.25 (4x faster).
5.  **Reboot**: Once it hits the speed limit (0.05), it resets to the slow 1.0s pace.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Starting delay (1 second)
delay_time = 1.0

while True:
    # Check for Speed Increase
    if btn.value() == 1:
        delay_time = delay_time / 2
        print("New Delay: " + str(delay_time))
        time.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
        
    # Boundary check
    if delay_time < 0.05:
        delay_time = 1.0
        print("Reset to Slow")
        
    # Execute Blink
    led.value(1)
    time.sleep(delay_time)
    led.value(0)
    time.sleep(delay_time)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Debounce**: A single tap might "cut in half" three or four times instantly because buttons vibrate. Always wait 0.3s after a press.
*   **No Exit Strategy**: If you don't reset, the code will eventually try to wait 0.0000001 seconds, which is too fast to see.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Additive Speed**: Change it to `delay_time = delay_time - 0.1` so the speed increases linearly instead of exponentially.
*   **Visual Speedometer**: add three colored LEDs that light up as you reach different speed tiers (Green=Slow, Yellow=Medium, Red=Fast).

---

## 1. Project 0206: Smart LED Patterns Switch

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore exclusive channel selection. Learn how to use a physical slide switch to toggle between two distinct groups of hardware (Group A vs Group B), ensuring only one side is active at a time.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Logical Exclusivity**: designing a system where two states cannot be active simultaneously.
*   **Hard-Switched States**: Using an input as a permanent mode selector rather than a temporary trigger.
*   **Group Organization**: logically bundling multiple outputs into a single "Bank".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Slide Switch
*   4 LEDs + Resistors
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Group A (LED 1&2)** | GP15, GP14 | Active when Switch is Left |
| **Group B (LED 3&4)** | GP13, GP12 | Active when Switch is Right |
| **Slide Switch** | GP11 | Input source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (if/then/else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin control)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: The direct pin state of the switch drives the execution branches.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Reset Outputs**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_gpio_write`. Set **GP15**, **GP14**, **GP13**, **GP12** to **LOW** (ensure off).

**B. Monitoring Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Check Switch**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if` with **Else**.
    *   Condition: From **Smart IO**, check if `pico_gpio_read` **GP11** is **HIGH**.

**C. Branch Execution**
4.  **Mode A (Switch Left)**:
    *   Inside **If**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** and **GP14** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** and **GP12** to **LOW**.
5.  **Mode B (Switch Right)**:
    *   Inside **Else**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** and **GP14** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** and **GP12** to **HIGH**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Read**: The Pico checks the physical position of the slide switch.
2.  **Logic**: If current is flowing through GP11, it follows the "If" branch.
3.  **Output**: The first two LEDs light up, while the others are turned off to ensure exclusivity.
4.  **Change**: When the user slides the switch, the Pico enters the "Else" branch on the very next cycle.
5.  **Result**: A reliable mode selector that manages multiple hardware channels instantly.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Inputs
sw = Pin(11, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Groups
bank_a = [Pin(15, Pin.OUT), Pin(14, Pin.OUT)]
bank_b = [Pin(13, Pin.OUT), Pin(12, Pin.OUT)]

while True:
    if sw.value() == 1:
        # Bank A ON
        bank_a[0].value(1); bank_a[1].value(1)
        bank_b[0].value(0); bank_b[1].value(0)
    else:
        # Bank B ON
        bank_a[0].value(0); bank_a[1].value(0)
        bank_b[0].value(1); bank_b[1].value(1)
    
    time.sleep(0.1) # Fast polling
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Floating State**: If you don't use the `else` block to turn the *other* group off, you will eventually end up with all 4 LEDs on.
*   **Wiring**: ensuring the common pin of the switch is connected to 3.3V correctly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Blink Modes**: instead of static ON/OFF, make Group A blink fast and Group B blink slow.
*   **Third State**: If using a 3-position switch, add an `else if` to have a "Center-Off" mode.

---

## 1. Project 0207: LED Patterns Alarm System

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore persistent state logic (The SR Latch). Learn how to create an alarm that "Persistence" through an event—triggering once and staying active until a secondary, deliberate "Reset" signal is received.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Latching State**: A variable that stays True until forced to False.
*   **Trigger vs. Reset**: Distinguishing between an input that starts a process and one that stops it.
*   **State Machines**: A simple logic flow with two distinct modes (Armed vs Tripped).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   2 Buttons (Trigger and Reset)
*   1 Red LED + Resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Trigger Button** | GP14 | Starts the alarm |
| **Reset Button** | GP13 | Clears the alarm |
| **Alarm LED** | GP15 | Stays ON when tripped |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (if/then)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (set variable)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin control)

### 7. Variables
*   **alarm_tripped** (Boolean): Tracks system state (Armed/Tripped).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Set State**:
    *   From **Variables**, set `alarm_tripped` to **False**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** (LED) to **LOW**.

**B. Monitoring Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Check Trigger**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**:
        *   From **Variables**, set `alarm_tripped` to **True**.
4.  **Check Reset**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP13** is **HIGH**:
        *   From **Variables**, set `alarm_tripped` to **False**.

**C. Output Logic**
5.  **Update Hardware**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if` with **Else**.
    *   Condition: If `alarm_tripped` is **True**.
    *   **Then**: From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   **Else**: From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: The Pico waits. `alarm_tripped` is false.
2.  **Trip**: A door opens, hitting the Trigger button for just a split second.
3.  **Latch**: The code sets `alarm_tripped` to true. Even though the button is now released, the variable stays true.
4.  **Respond**: The LED turns on and stays lit.
5.  **Reset**: The security guard presses the Reset button. `alarm_tripped` becomes false, and the LED turns off.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn_trigger = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_reset = Pin(13, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

# Internal State
alarm_active = False

while True:
    # Handle Trigger
    if btn_trigger.value() == 1:
        alarm_active = True
        print("ALARM TRIPPED!")
        
    # Handle Reset
    if btn_reset.value() == 1:
        alarm_active = False
        print("Alarm Cleared.")
        
    # Apply State to Hardware
    if alarm_active:
        led.value(1)
    else:
        led.value(0)
        
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Button Collision**: If both buttons are held at once, the code order determines the outcome (usually the second check, Reset, will win).
*   **No Variables**: Trying to do this without a variable (direct pin control) means the LED would only be on while the trigger is held, which isn't a persistent alarm.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Audio Alarm**: add a buzzer that beeps only when the alarm is tripped.
*   **Tripped Indicator**: adding a Green LED that is ON when safe and turns OFF when the Red LED is tripped.

---

## 1. Project 0208: The LED Patterns Game

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore precision timing and input window detection. Learn how to implement "Stop the Light," where a user must synchronize a physical action (button press) with a specific state in a fast-moving sequence.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Target Calculation**: Checking if an input occurs during a specific sub-interval of a sequence.
*   **Game State Feedback**: providing immediate visual confirmation of Win/Loss.
*   **Variable Delays**: Speeding up the sequence as the player gets better.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 Button
*   3 LEDs (e.g., Red, Yellow, Green)
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED 1 (Side)** | GP15 | Normal light |
| **LED 2 (Middle)** | GP14 | THE TARGET LIGHT |
| **LED 3 (Side)** | GP13 | Normal light |
| **Input Button** | GP12 | Press to "Catch" the light |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Loops, drag `controls_repeat_ext`** (sequence loop)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (checking if user caught it)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Logic handled by flow control.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Sequence Loop**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **LED 1 (Side)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**, **GP14/GP13** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.15** s.
    *   **Check Input**: From **Logic**, If **Button** (GP12) Pressed: Print "MISS!".
3.  **LED 2 (Target)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP14** to **HIGH**, **GP15/GP13** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Logic**, If **Button** (GP12) Pressed:
        *   Print "WINNNER!".
        *   Flash all LEDs 5 times (0.05s).
        *   Wait 2s.
    *   **Wait**: from **Smart IO**, wait **0.15** s.
4.  **LED 3 (Side)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** to **HIGH**, **GP15/GP14** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.15** s.
    *   **Check Input**: From **Logic**, If **Button** (GP12) Pressed: Print "MISS!".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Run**: The lights cycle: 1... 2... 3... 1... 2... 3... very fast.
2.  **Target**: The middle light (GP14) is only on for 150 milliseconds.
3.  **Act**: The user taps the button.
4.  **Math**: If the tap happened during the 150ms window of GP14, the "Win" block fires.
5.  **Result**: An interactive skill game that tests human reaction speed.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
leds = [machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT), 
        machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT), 
        machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)]
btn = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    for i in range(3):
        # Light up the current LED
        for j in range(3):
            leds[j].value(1 if i == j else 0)
            
        # Check for button press in a small loop to catch it
        start_wait = utime.ticks_ms()
        while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start_wait) < 150:
            if btn.value():
                if i == 1: # Middle LED is index 1
                    print("YOU WIN!")
                    for _ in range(5):
                        for l in leds: l.value(1)
                        utime.sleep(0.05)
                        for l in leds: l.value(0)
                        utime.sleep(0.05)
                else:
                    print("TOO EARLY/LATE")
                    utime.sleep(1)
                utime.sleep(1) # Gap between games
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Long Delays**: If you use a single `time.sleep(0.15)` block, the Pico is "blind" to the button while it sleeps. You must use a tight checking loop (like the one in the code above) to catch the press.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Difficulty Scaling**: every time you win, reduce the wait time from 150ms to 130ms.
*   **Score Tracker**: count how many wins you can get in a row before missing once.

## 1. Project 0209: Automated LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore proportional brightness control (Ambient Match). Learn how to use a Light Sensor (LDR) to read environmental data and map it directly to an LED's PWM duty cycle to create "Self-Adjusting" lighting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Analog Input Calibration**: converting a raw light value into a percentage.
*   **Pulse Width Modulation (PWM)**: Simulating different brightness levels on a digital pin.
*   **Proportional Logic**: designing a relationship where "More Light outside = More Light on the Pico".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Photoresistor (LDR)
*   10k Ohm resistor (Voltage Divider)
*   1 LED + resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Light Sensor** | GP26 (ADC0) | Measures room light |
| **Output LED** | GP15 | Adjusted via PWM |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `pico_analog_read`** (read ADC)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (set variable)
*   **from Math, drag `math_map`** (convert range)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `pico_pwm_write`** (set Brightness)

### 7. Variables
*   **light_level** (Number): Raw ADC input (0-65535).
*   **led_brightness** (Number): Mapped duty cycle (0-100% or 0-65535).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Setup Hardware**:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, initialize **GP15** as PWM.

**B. Main Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Read Sensor**:
    *   From **Variables**, set `light_level`.
    *   Snap **Smart Sensors** `pico_analog_read` (GP26) into it.
4.  **Process Data**:
    *   From **Variables**, set `led_brightness`.
    *   Snap **Math** `math_map` block:
        *   Input: `light_level`.
        *   From Low/High: **0** to **65535**.
        *   To Low/High: **0** to **100** (or max PWM).
5.  **Output**:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, set **GP15** PWM Duty to `led_brightness`.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.1** s.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Read**: The Pico sees the sun is shining brightly (High ADC value).
2.  **Math**: The mapping block converts this "Bright" reading into a "100%" brightness request.
3.  **Act**: The LED turns on full power so it can be seen under the sun.
4.  **Change**: You put your hand over the sensor. The Pico sees "Dark" (Low ADC).
5.  **Dim**: The mapping block requests "10%". The LED dims instantly so it doesn't hurt your eyes in the dark.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware
ldr = machine.ADC(machine.Pin(26))
led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
led.freq(1000)

while True:
    # Read (0-65535)
    raw_val = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Map raw value to PWM duty cycle
    # In bright light, we want high brightness.
    # Adjust thresholds based on your room's baseline.
    duty = int(raw_val)
    
    # Clamp to ensure it stays in range
    if duty > 65535: duty = 65535
    if duty < 0: duty = 0
    
    led.duty_u16(duty)
    
    # Status
    # print("Light: " + str(raw_val) + " -> Power: " + str(duty))
    utime.sleep(0.1)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Inversion**: If the LED gets BRIGHTER when it's DARK, it's acting as a night-light. To fix this, swap the map outputs: from [0-65535] to [100%-0%].
*   **ADC Pin**: Only pins 26, 27, and 28 support analog reading.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Night Light Mode**: Swap the logic so the LED is only ON when the room is dark.
*   **Transition Smoothing**: create a variable that slowly crawls towards the target brightness instead of jumping instantly.

## 1. Project 0210: Mastering LED Patterns

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore temporal-spatial illusions (Persistence of Vision - POV). Learn how to flash a row of LEDs at extremely high speeds (milliseconds) so that they appear to "Draw" a shape or letter in mid-air when physically waved.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistence of Vision**: How the human eye creates a continuous image from fast flashes.
*   **Column Scanning**: Drawing one vertical line of an image at a time.
*   **Timing Criticality**: Using the smallest possible delays (e.g. 5-10ms) for high-speed synchronization.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   5 LEDs + Resistors (placed in a strict vertical row)
*   Hands (to wave the wand)
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED Row (Top to Bottom)** | GP15, 14, 13, 12, 11 | The "Pixel" Column |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait_ms`** (wait in milliseconds)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Hardcoded timing sequences.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Drawing Frame**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Column 1 (Lines)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15, 14, 13, 12, 11** to **HIGH**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **5** ms.
3.  **Column 2 (Bridge)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP13** (Middle) to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15, 14, 12, 11** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **5** ms.
4.  **Column 3 (Lines)**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15, 14, 13, 12, 11** to **HIGH**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **5** ms.

**B. Reset Gap**
5.  **Blank Frame**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **All LEDs** to **LOW**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **20** ms.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Static**: While sitting still, the LEDs look like they are just flashing randomly and very fast.
2.  **Motion**: The user holds the breadboard and waves it quickly from left to right.
3.  **Persistence**: Because the LEDs change state while they are physically moving through space, your eyes "smear" the flashes into a single horizontal image.
4.  **Result**: The letter "H" appears floating in the air.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Static**: While sitting still, the LEDs look like they are just flashing randomly and very fast.
2.  **Motion**: The user holds the breadboard and waves it quickly from left to right.
3.  **Persistence**: Because the LEDs change state while they are physically moving through space, your eyes "smear" the flashes into a single horizontal image.
4.  **Result**: The letter "H" appears floating in the air.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

# Vertical LED strip
pixels = [Pin(15, Pin.OUT), Pin(14, Pin.OUT), Pin(13, Pin.OUT), 
          Pin(12, Pin.OUT), Pin(11, Pin.OUT)]

def set_col(p1, p2, p3, p4, p5):
    pixels[0].value(p1)
    pixels[1].value(p2)
    pixels[2].value(p3)
    pixels[3].value(p4)
    pixels[4].value(p5)
    time.sleep_ms(5)

while True:
    # Drawing 'H'
    set_col(1, 1, 1, 1, 1) # Full line
    set_col(0, 0, 1, 0, 0) # Middle dot
    set_col(1, 1, 1, 1, 1) # Full line
    
    # Drawing 'I'
    set_col(0, 0, 0, 0, 0) # Gap
    set_col(1, 0, 0, 0, 1) # Top and Bottom
    set_col(1, 1, 1, 1, 1) # Center stem
    set_col(1, 0, 0, 0, 1) # Top and Bottom
    
    # Reset
    set_col(0, 0, 0, 0, 0)
    time.sleep_ms(30) # Wait before repeating word
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Too Slow**: If you wait 50ms instead of 5ms, you will just see a series of dots instead of a connected image.
*   **Wobble**: You must wave the Pico at a consistent speed for the image to look correctly proportioned.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Custom Shapes**: draw a smiley face by mapping out a 5x5 grid.
*   **Speed Switch**: add a button that switches between 5ms, 10ms, and 15ms so you can match the speed to different arm-waving strengths.

# BATCH 22: Button Logic 2 (Projects 0211-0220)

## 1. Project 0211: Introduction to Button Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore real-time polling visualization (The State Check). Learn how to continuously monitor a digital input pin and report its state across the serial interface, observing the high-speed interaction between physical hardware and software logic.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Digital Polling**: checking a pin state many times per second.
*   **Strings/Text Output**: printing different messages based on logic results.
*   **Rising and Falling Inputs**: understanding that a button is a state-changing device.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 Pushbutton
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Input Switch** | GP14 | Monitored for state changes |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (if/then/else)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pin)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (printing text)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: This is a direct conditional reporting script.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring Loop**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Check Input**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if` with **Else**.
    *   Condition: From **Smart IO**, `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.

**B. Feedback Phase**
3.  **Active State**:
    *   Inside **If**: From **Text**, print "Button Status: PRESSED".
4.  **Idle State**:
    *   Inside **Else**: From **Text**, print "Button Status: RELEASED".
5.  **Throttle**:
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.1** s.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters the loop and sees the button is up.
2.  **Logic**: It follows the "Else" path and prints "RELEASED".
3.  **Action**: You press and hold the button.
4.  **Change**: On the next cycle, the Pico sees the HIGH signal and follows the "If" path, printing "PRESSED".
5.  **Result**: A live scrolling log of your physical interaction with the hardware.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        print("PRESSED")
    else:
        print("RELEASED")
    
    time.sleep(0.1)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Else**: If you don't use an else block, the Pico won't tell you when the button is released; the "Pressed" text will just stop appearing, providing less information.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Fast Polling**: Remove the 0.1s wait and see how thousands of messages appear in a single second!
*   **LED Sync**: turn an LED on when pressed and off when released alongside the text.

## 1. Project 0212: Blinking Button Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore edge detection (The Single Shot). Learn how to implement logic that triggers an action exactly once per press, regardless of how long the button is held, demonstrating "Rising Edge" detection.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Edge Triggering**: reacting only to the moment a signal goes from 0 to 1.
*   **State Locking**: using a "Wait Until Released" block to pause execution.
*   **Single-Shot Events**: ensuring a recurring loop doesn't repeat an action unintentionally.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 Pushbutton
*   1 LED + resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Trigger Btn** | GP14 | Single pulse input |
| **Burst LED** | GP15 | Visual feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (detecting event)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set LED)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_wait_until`** (edge control)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Hardware state blocking.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring Loop**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Trigger Detection**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`.
    *   Condition: From **Smart IO**, `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.

**B. Single-Shot Execution**
3.  **Perform Action**:
    *   Inside **If**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.1** s.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
4.  **Wait for Release (Lock)**:
    *   Inside **If** (after blink):
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_wait_until`.
    *   Condition: **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **LOW**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Press**: You push the button.
2.  **Action**: The Pico sees the TRUE signal, enters the IF block, and blinks the LED once.
3.  **Stall**: The Pico hits the "Wait Until" block. It will stay there forever as long as your finger is still on the button.
4.  **Release**: You let go. The Pico sees the button is LOW, exits the "Wait Until", and finishes the loop.
5.  **Result**: Even if you hold the button for an hour, the LED only blinks once at the very start.
6.  **Outcome**: Mastery of precise input timing controls.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        # 1. Action
        led.value(1); time.sleep(0.1); led.value(0)
        
        # 2. Wait until finger is removed
        while btn.value() == 1:
            time.sleep(0.01)
            
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wait in Forever**: If you put the "Wait Until Released" block outside the IF statement, the whole Pico will freeze the moment you start the program because it's waiting for you to release a button you haven't even pressed yet!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Double Blink**: make the LED flash twice on every press.
*   **Inverted Single-Shot**: make the blink happen when you **release** the button rather than when you press it.

## 1. Project 0213: Manual Button Logic Control

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore logic gates (The AND Gate). Learn how to implement a conditional check that requires multiple inputs to be active simultaneously, demonstrating how digital systems perform security or safety checks.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **AND Logic**: a result that is only True if all inputs are True.
*   **Conditional Nesting**: placing one `if` inside another or using an `AND` math block.
*   **Concurrent Polling**: reading two pins in the same logic step.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   2 Buttons (A and B)
*   1 LED + resistor
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP14 | First part of key |
| **Button B** | GP13 | Second part of key |
| **Safe LED** | GP15 | Results indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (if/then/else)
*   **from Logic, drag `logic_operation`** (AND)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read Pins)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set LED)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Direct logic gate.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring Loop**
1.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Logic Gate**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if` with **Else**.
    *   From **Logic**, drag the `and` block into the condition slot.

**B. Define Conditions**
3.  **Input A**:
    *   Snap into Left side of AND: **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.
4.  **Input B**:
    *   Snap into Right side of AND: **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP13** is **HIGH**.

**C. Output Phase**
5.  **Access Granted**:
    *   Inside **If**: From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
6.  **Access Denied**:
    *   Inside **Else**: From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Action**: You press Button A. The LED stays OFF because AND requires BOTH.
2.  **Action**: You let go of A and press B. The LED stays OFF.
3.  **Action**: You use two fingers to press A AND B at the same time.
4.  **Logic**: The AND condition becomes True. The Pico follows the "If" branch.
5.  **Result**: The LED turns ON.
6.  **Outcome**: Simulating a high-security "Two-Key" launch system or safety machinery.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn_a = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_b = Pin(13, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if btn_a.value() == 1 and btn_b.value() == 1:
        led.value(1)
    else:
        led.value(0)
    
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **OR vs AND**: If you accidentally use the `or` block, the light will turn on if you press EITHER button, which is less secure.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Triple AND**: Add a third button. The LED only turns on if all three are down.
*   **NAND Gate**: Make the LED stay ON normally, and turn OFF only if both buttons are pressed.

## 1. Project 0214: Button Logic Sequences

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore sequence validation (The Combo Lock). Learn how to implement a state machine that tracks a specific order of inputs (A -> A -> B) and only provides a "Success" signal if the timing and order are perfect.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sequential Logic**: actions that depend on what happened previously.
*   **State Tracking**: using a variable to remember where the user is in a sequence.
*   **Reset Logic**: clearing progress if a mistake is made.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   2 Buttons (A and B)
*   1 Green LED (Success)
*   1 Red LED (Fault)
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button A** | GP14 | Primary Input |
| **Button B** | GP13 | Secondary Input |
| **Green LED** | GP15 | Success |
| **Red LED** | GP12 | Error |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (tracking state)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (checking sequence)

### 7. Variables
*   **step** (Number): Progress tracker (0=Start, 1=A pressed, 2=A twice).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Reset**:
    *   From **Variables**, set `step` to **0**.
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** (Green) and **GP12** (Red) to **LOW**.

**B. Main Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.

**C. Input A Logic (The Key)**
3.  **Check Button A**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**:
    *   **Progress Check**:
        *   If `step` == 0: Set `step` to **1**. Print "1/3".
        *   Else If `step` == 1: Set `step` to **2**. Print "2/3".
        *   Else: Set `step` to **0**. Pulse Red LED (Error).
    *   **Wait Release**: From **Loops**, `wait_until` GP14 is **LOW**.

**D. Input B Logic (The Enter Key)**
4.  **Check Button B**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP13** is **HIGH**:
    *   **Unlock Check**:
        *   If `step` == 2:
            *   Print "ACCESS GRANTED".
            *   Pulse Green LED (2s).
            *   Set `step` to **0**.
        *   Else:
            *   Print "ACCESS DENIED".
            *   Pulse Red LED (1s).
            *   Set `step` to **0**.
    *   **Wait Release**: From **Loops**, `wait_until` GP13 is **LOW**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Step is 0.
2.  **Input**: You press A. Step becomes 1.
3.  **Input**: You press A again. Step becomes 2.
4.  **Input**: You press B.
5.  **Verdict**: The Pico sees `step` is 2, so the "Success" branch triggers.
6.  **Mistake**: If you press B at Step 0, the Red LED immediately flashes and you have to start over.
7.  **Result**: An interactive security system protected by a 3-part code.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn_a = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_b = Pin(13, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
led_g = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
led_r = Pin(12, Pin.OUT)

step = 0

while True:
    # Check A
    if btn_a.value() == 1:
        if step == 0: 
            step = 1; print("1/3")
        elif step == 1: 
            step = 2; print("2/3")
        else:
            step = 0; led_r.value(1); time.sleep(0.5); led_r.value(0)
        while btn_a.value() == 1: time.sleep(0.01) # Wait release
        
    # Check B
    if btn_b.value() == 1:
        if step == 2:
            print("OPEN!"); led_g.value(1); time.sleep(2); led_g.value(0)
            step = 0
        else:
            print("WRONG!"); led_r.value(1); time.sleep(1); led_r.value(0)
            step = 0
        while btn_b.value() == 1: time.sleep(0.01)
        
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Reset**: forgetting to set `step = 0` after a mistake means a user could eventually find the right code by random trial and error.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Longer Code**: Make the code 5 steps long (A-B-A-B-A).
*   **Time Limit**: Clear the sequence if the user takes more than 5 seconds between buttons.

---

## 1. Project 0215: Interactive Button Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore accumulator logic (The Counter with Reset). Learn how to use one input to increment a variable and a second input to perform a "Master Reset," providing a foundation for scoreboards and industrial tally systems.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Accumulation**: `Count = Count + 1`.
*   **Instant Resets**: Overwriting a variable with 0 regardless of its previous value.
*   **UI Feedback**: Printing live values to provide a "Virtual Display."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   2 Buttons (Count and Reset)
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Count Btn** | GP14 | Adds one to total |
| **Reset Btn** | GP13 | Clears total |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Variables, drag `variables_set`** (tracking count)
*   **from Math, drag `math_arithmetic`** (addition)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (listening)

### 7. Variables
*   **tally** (Number): The count total.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Reset**:
    *   From **Variables**, set `tally` to **0**.
    *   From **Text**, print "Ready".

**B. Main Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.

**C. Increment Logic**
3.  **Button A (Add)**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**:
        *   From **Variables**, set `tally` to **Math** (`tally` + 1).
        *   From **Text**, print "Count: " + `tally`.
        *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.3** s (Debounce).

**D. Reset Logic**
4.  **Button B (Clear)**:
    *   From **Logic**, If **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP13** is **HIGH**:
        *   From **Variables**, set `tally` to **0**.
        *   From **Text**, print "RESET".
        *   from **Smart IO**, wait **0.5** s.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Manual Tally**: You press Button A. The screen says "1". You press again. "2".
2.  **Tracking**: You keep counting until you reach 50.
3.  **Correction**: You made a mistake or want to start over.
4.  **Clear**: You press Button B.
5.  **Logic**: The Pico immediately changes the `tally` variable from 50 back to 0.
6.  **Result**: A digital tally system that can handle thousands of inputs without losing its place.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

btn_count = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)
btn_reset = Pin(13, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

tally = 0

print("System Ready.")

while True:
    # 1. Increment
    if btn_count.value() == 1:
        tally += 1
        print("Count: " + str(tally))
        time.sleep(0.3) # Wait for release/debounce
        
    # 2. Reset
    if btn_reset.value() == 1:
        tally = 0
        print("RESET")
        time.sleep(0.5)
        
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **No Debounce**: If you click the button once but the Pico counts "2" or "3", it's because buttons vibrate. Ensure the 0.3s wait is inside the if block.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Countdown**: add a third button that subtracts (`tally = tally - 1`).
*   **Goal Detection**: print "TARGET REACHED!" when the counter hits exactly 10.

## 1. Project 0216: Smart Button Logic Switch

### 2. Learning Objective
Explore temporal state transitions (The Exit Delay). Learn how to implement a "Delayed Off" sequence where the system acknowledges a shutdown command (Button Press) but maintains an active state (LED On) for a specific duration before final deactivation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Off-Delay Timer**: Delaying the transition from ON to OFF after an input trigger.
*   **State Latency**: introducing a purely software-driven "Active" period that doesn't rely on physical holding.
*   **User Safety Logic**: Simulating exit lighting found in cars or hallways.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   1 LED + resistor
*   1 Button
*   Breadboard and jumper wires

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic Button** | GP14 | Press to trigger exit delay |
| **Exit Light** | GP15 | Stays ON for 5s after press |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (forever do)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (detecting click)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (set Pin)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_wait`** (wait)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Linear timed sequence.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Safety State**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.

**B. Control Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Detect Request**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `controls_if`. Condition: **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` **GP14** is **HIGH**.

**C. Exit Sequence**
4.  **Confirm**:
    *   Inside **If**: From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **HIGH**.
    *   From **Text**, print "Shutdown Sequence Started...".
5.  **Hold**:
    *   from **Smart IO**, wait **5** seconds.
6.  **Shutdown**:
    *   From **Smart IO**, set **GP15** to **LOW**.
    *   From **Text**, print "System OFF".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: The light is off.
2.  **Trigger**: You press the button once.
3.  **Hold**: The Pico immediately turns the light ON (or keeps it ON) and hits the 5-second "Wait" block.
4.  **Timer**: The light illuminates your "path" for exactly 5 seconds.
5.  **Finish**: The Pico turns the light OFF and goes back to waiting for the next press.

### 10. Generated Code
``python
from machine import Pin
import time

led = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
btn = Pin(14, Pin.IN, Pin.PULL_DOWN)

while True:
    if btn.value() == 1:
        # User requested off - start delay
        led.value(1)
        print("Exit delay active: 5s")
        time.sleep(5)
        
        # Turn off after delay
        led.value(0)
        print("Light turned off.")
        
    time.sleep(0.01)
``

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Button Spam**: If you press the button during the 5-second wait, nothing happens because the Pico is "blocked" by the `sleep` command.
*   **Initial State**: If the problem requires the light to be ON first, you would need a toggle variable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Warning Blink**: Make the LED flash during the final 1 second of the delay so the user knows it's about to go dark.
*   **Cancelable Delay**: Try to make it so pressing the button AGAIN during the 5s delay turns the light off immediately.
